### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 2N people in a row, each wearing clothes of color A_i
2. There are exactly N colors (1 to N), and each color appears exactly twice
3. For each color i (from 1 to N), we need to check if there is exactly one person between the two people wearing that color
4. We need to count how many colors satisfy this condition

## Key Insight

For a color i to have exactly one person between the two people wearing it:
- If the positions of the two people wearing color i are at indices p and q (0-indexed), then |p - q| should be exactly 2
- This means the positions should be like (k, k+2) for some k

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read the input: N and the array A of length 2N
2. For each color from 1 to N:
   - Find the two positions where this color appears
   - Calculate the distance between these two positions
   - Check if the distance is exactly 2 (meaning exactly one person between them)
3. Count how many colors satisfy this condition

## Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

count = 0

# For each color from 1 to N
for color in range(1, N + 1):
    # Find the two positions where this color appears
    positions = []
    for i in range(2 * N):
        if A[i] == color:
            positions.append(i)
    
    # Check if there's exactly one person between them
    # This means the distance between positions should be exactly 2
    if len(positions) == 2:  # Each color appears exactly twice
        distance = abs(positions[0] - positions[1])
        if distance == 2:
            count += 1

print(count)
```

## Verification with examples

**Sample 1**: N=3, A=[1,2,1,3,2,3]
- Color 1: positions 0,2 → distance = 2 → satisfies condition
- Color 2: positions 1,4 → distance = 3 → doesn't satisfy
- Color 3: positions 3,5 → distance = 2 → satisfies condition
- Count = 2 ✓

**Sample 2**: N=2, A=[1,1,2,2]
- Color 1: positions 0,1 → distance = 1 → doesn't satisfy
- Color 2: positions 2,3 → distance = 1 → doesn't satisfy
- Count = 0 ✓

**Sample 3**: N=4, A=[4,3,2,3,2,1,4,1]
- Color 1: positions 5,7 → distance = 2 → satisfies
- Color 2: positions 2,4 → distance = 2 → satisfies  
- Color 3: positions 1,3 → distance = 2 → satisfies
- Color 4: positions 0,6 → distance = 6 → doesn't satisfy
- Count = 3 ✓

The solution works correctly for all test cases.

    # This means the distance between positions should be exactly 2
    if len(positions) == 2:  # Each color appears exactly twice

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 2N people in a row, each wearing clothes of color A_i
2. There are exactly N colors (1 to N), and each color appears exactly twice
3. We need to count how many colors i have exactly one person between the two people wearing that color

## Key Insight

For a color i to have exactly one person between the two people wearing it:
- If the first person wearing color i is at position p, then the second person must be at position p+2
- This means the two positions for color i must differ by exactly 2

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read the input: N and the array A of 2N elements
2. For each color i from 1 to N:
   - Find the two positions where color i appears
   - Check if the difference between these positions is exactly 2
3. Count how many colors satisfy this condition

## Implementation Strategy

1. Create a dictionary to store the positions of each color
2. For each color, get its two positions
3. Check if the absolute difference between positions is exactly 2
4. Count such colors

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:** [1, 2, 1, 3, 2, 3]
- Color 1: positions 0, 2 → difference = 2 → satisfies condition
- Color 2: positions 1, 4 → difference = 3 → doesn't satisfy
- Color 3: positions 3, 5 → difference = 2 → satisfies condition
- Answer: 2

**Example 2:** [1, 1, 2, 2]
- Color 1: positions 0, 1 → difference = 1 → doesn't satisfy
- Color 2: positions 2, 3 → difference = 1 → doesn't satisfy
- Answer: 0

**Example 3:** [4, 3, 2, 3, 2, 1, 4, 1]
- Color 1: positions 5, 7 → difference = 2 → satisfies
- Color 2: positions 2, 4 → difference = 2 → satisfies  
- Color 3: positions 1, 3 → difference = 2 → satisfies
- Color 4: positions 0, 6 → difference = 6 → doesn't satisfy
- Answer: 3

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Dictionary to store positions of each color
color_positions = {}

# Fill the positions for each color
for i in range(2 * n):
    color = a[i]
    if color not in color_positions:
        color_positions[color] = []
    color_positions[color].append(i)

# Count colors that have exactly one person between them
count = 0
for color in range(1, n + 1):
    positions = color_positions[color]
    pos1, pos2 = positions[0], positions[1]
    # Check if there's exactly one person between them
    if abs(pos2 - pos1) == 2:
        count += 1

print(count)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 2N people in a row, each wearing clothes of color A_i
2. There are exactly N colors (1 to N), and each color appears exactly twice
3. We need to count how many colors i have exactly one person between the two people wearing that color

## Key Insight

For a color i to have exactly one person between the two people wearing it:
- If the first person wearing color i is at position j, the second person must be at position j+2
- This means the two positions must be exactly 2 apart (with one person in between)

## Algorithm

1. Read the input: N and the array A of 2N elements
2. For each color i from 1 to N:
   - Find the two positions where color i appears
   - Check if the absolute difference between these positions is exactly 2
   - If yes, increment our counter
3. Return the counter

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read N
2. Read the array A of 2N elements
3. For each color from 1 to N:
   - Find the indices where this color appears
   - Calculate the distance between these indices
   - If distance is exactly 2, count it

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: N=3, A=[1,2,1,3,2,3]
- Color 1: positions 0,2 → distance = 2 → satisfies condition ✓
- Color 2: positions 1,4 → distance = 3 → doesn't satisfy
- Color 3: positions 3,5 → distance = 2 → satisfies condition ✓
- Answer: 2

**Example 2**: N=2, A=[1,1,2,2]
- Color 1: positions 0,1 → distance = 1 → doesn't satisfy
- Color 2: positions 2,3 → distance = 1 → doesn't satisfy
- Answer: 0

**Example 3**: N=4, A=[4,3,2,3,2,1,4,1]
- Color 1: positions 5,7 → distance = 2 → satisfies ✓
- Color 2: positions 2,4 → distance = 2 → satisfies ✓
- Color 3: positions 1,3 → distance = 2 → satisfies ✓
- Color 4: positions 0,6 → distance = 6 → doesn't satisfy
- Answer: 3

```python
N = int(input())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

count = 0

# For each color from 1 to N
for color in range(1, N + 1):
    # Find positions where this color appears
    positions = []
    for i in range(2 * N):
        if A[i] == color:
            positions.append(i)
    
    # Check if there's exactly one person between them
    # This means the distance between positions should be exactly 2
    if len(positions) == 2:  # Should always be true by constraint
        distance = positions[1] - positions[0]
        if distance == 2:
            count += 1

print(count)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 2N people in a row, each wearing clothes of color A_i
2. There are N colors (1 to N), and each color appears exactly twice
3. We need to count how many colors i have exactly one person between the two people wearing that color

## Key Insight

For a color i to satisfy the condition, the two people wearing color i must be at positions that have exactly one person between them. This means if one person is at position j, the other must be at position j+2.

So for color i, if the positions of the two people are p1 and p2 (with p1 < p2), we need p2 - p1 = 2.

## Solution Steps

1. Read the input: N and the array A of 2N values
2. For each color from 1 to N, find the positions where it appears
3. Check if the difference between the two positions is exactly 2
4. Count how many colors satisfy this condition

## Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

# For each color from 1 to N, find the positions where it appears
count = 0

for color in range(1, N + 1):
    positions = []
    for i in range(2 * N):
        if A[i] == color:
            positions.append(i + 1)  # Convert to 1-based indexing
    
    # Check if there's exactly one person between the two people
    # This means the positions should differ by exactly 2
    if len(positions) == 2:
        if positions[1] - positions[0] == 2:
            count += 1

print(count)
```

## Verification with examples

**Sample 1:** N=3, A=[1,2,1,3,2,3]
- Color 1: positions at 1 and 3 → difference = 2 → satisfies condition ✓
- Color 2: positions at 2 and 5 → difference = 3 → doesn't satisfy
- Color 3: positions at 4 and 6 → difference = 2 → satisfies condition ✓
- Answer: 2 ✓

**Sample 2:** N=2, A=[1,1,2,2]
- Color 1: positions at 1 and 2 → difference = 1 → doesn't satisfy
- Color 2: positions at 3 and 4 → difference = 1 → doesn't satisfy
- Answer: 0 ✓

**Sample 3:** N=4, A=[4,3,2,3,2,1,4,1]
- Color 1: positions at 6 and 8 → difference = 2 → satisfies ✓
- Color 2: positions at 3 and 5 → difference = 2 → satisfies ✓
- Color 3: positions at 2 and 4 → difference = 2 → satisfies ✓
- Color 4: positions at 1 and 7 → difference = 6 → doesn't satisfy
- Answer: 3 ✓

The solution works correctly for all test cases.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 2N people in a row, each wearing clothes of color A_i
2. There are exactly N colors (1 to N), and each color appears exactly twice
3. We need to count how many colors i satisfy the condition that there is exactly one person between the two people wearing color i
4. "Exactly one person between" means the two people with color i are at positions j and j+2 (distance 2 apart)

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Identify what "exactly one person between" means
- If two people with color i are at positions p and q, then there's exactly one person between them if |p - q| = 2
- This means they are separated by exactly one person

### Step 2: Algorithm approach
1. Read the input: N and the array A of 2N elements
2. For each color i from 1 to N, find the positions where it appears
3. Check if the two positions have exactly one person between them (distance = 2)
4. Count how many colors satisfy this condition

### Step 3: Implementation details
- We need to store the positions of each color
- For each color, get its two positions and check if their difference is exactly 2
- Note: since we're dealing with 1-indexed positions, if positions are p and q, then |p - q| = 2 means there's exactly one person between them

### Step 4: Trace through examples

**Example 1:** N=3, A = [1,2,1,3,2,3]
- Color 1: positions 1, 3 → |1-3| = 2 ✓ (one person between)
- Color 2: positions 2, 5 → |2-5| = 3 ✗ (two people between)
- Color 3: positions 4, 6 → |4-6| = 2 ✓ (one person between)
- Answer: 2

**Example 2:** N=2, A = [1,1,2,2]
- Color 1: positions 1, 2 → |1-2| = 1 ✗ (no one between)
- Color 2: positions 3, 4 → |3-4| = 1 ✗ (no one between)
- Answer: 0

**Example 3:** N=4, A = [4,3,2,3,2,1,4,1]
- Color 1: positions 6, 8 → |6-8| = 2 ✓
- Color 2: positions 3, 5 → |3-5| = 2 ✓
- Color 3: positions 2, 4 → |2-4| = 2 ✓
- Color 4: positions 1, 7 → |1-7| = 6 ✗
- Answer: 3

## Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create a dictionary to store positions of each color
color_positions = {}

# Fill the positions for each color
for i in range(2 * N):
    color = A[i]
    if color not in color_positions:
        color_positions[color] = []
    color_positions[color].append(i + 1)  # Convert to 1-indexed

# Count colors that have exactly one person between them
count = 0
for color in range(1, N + 1):
    positions = color_positions[color]
    # There should be exactly two positions for each color
    if len(positions) == 2:
        pos1, pos2 = positions
        if abs(pos1 - pos2) == 2:
            count += 1

print(count)
```

The solution works as follows:
1. Read input values
2. For each color, store the positions (1-indexed) where it appears
3. For each color from 1 to N, check if the two positions differ by exactly 2
4. If yes, increment the counter
5. Print the final count

Time complexity: O(N) since we process each element once and each color has exactly two positions.
Space complexity: O(N) for storing the positions.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 2N people in a row, each wearing clothes of color A_i
2. There are N colors from 1 to N, and each color appears exactly twice
3. We need to count how many colors i have exactly one person between the two people wearing that color

## Key Insight

For a color i to have exactly one person between the two people wearing it:
- If the two people wearing color i are at positions p and q (where p < q), then q - p = 2
- This means the second person is exactly 2 positions after the first person

## Algorithm Steps

1. Read the input: N and the array A of 2N elements
2. For each color from 1 to N:
   - Find the two positions where this color appears
   - Check if the difference between these positions is exactly 2
3. Count how many colors satisfy this condition

## Implementation Strategy

1. Create a dictionary to store the positions of each color
2. For each color, get its two positions
3. Check if the absolute difference between positions is 2
4. Count valid colors

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
# Read input
N = int(input())
A = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Dictionary to store positions for each color
color_positions = {}

# Fill the positions dictionary
for i in range(2 * N):
    color = A[i]
    if color not in color_positions:
        color_positions[color] = []
    color_positions[color].append(i)

# Count how many colors have exactly one person between them
count = 0
for color in range(1, N + 1):
    positions = color_positions[color]
    pos1, pos2 = positions[0], positions[1]
    
    # Check if there's exactly one person between them
    # This means the difference should be exactly 2
    if pos2 - pos1 == 2:
        count += 1

print(count)
```

## Verification with Examples

**Sample 1**: N=3, A=[1,2,1,3,2,3]
- Color 1: positions 0,2 → difference = 2 ✓ (valid)
- Color 2: positions 1,4 → difference = 3 ✗ (invalid) 
- Color 3: positions 3,5 → difference = 2 ✓ (valid)
- Answer: 2 ✓

**Sample 2**: N=2, A=[1,1,2,2]
- Color 1: positions 0,1 → difference = 1 ✗ (invalid)
- Color 2: positions 2,3 → difference = 1 ✗ (invalid)
- Answer: 0 ✓

**Sample 3**: N=4, A=[4,3,2,3,2,1,4,1]
- Color 1: positions 5,7 → difference = 2 ✓ (valid)
- Color 2: positions 2,4 → difference = 2 ✓ (valid)
- Color 3: positions 1,3 → difference = 2 ✓ (valid)
- Color 4: positions 0,6 → difference = 6 ✗ (invalid)
- Answer: 3 ✓

The solution works correctly for all test cases.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The Smart Beaver from ABBYY came up with another splendid problem for the ABBYY Cup participants! This time the Beaver invites the contest participants to check out a problem on sorting documents by their subjects. Let's describe the problem:

You've got some training set of documents. For each document you know its subject. The subject in this problem is an integer from 1 to 3. Each of these numbers has a physical meaning. For instance, all documents with subject 3 are about trade.

You can download the training set of documents at the following link: http://download4.abbyy.com/a2/X2RZ2ZWXBG5VYWAL61H76ZQM/train.zip. The archive contains three directories with names "1", "2", "3". Directory named "1" contains documents on the 1-st subject, directory "2" contains documents on the 2-nd subject, and directory "3" contains documents on the 3-rd subject. Each document corresponds to exactly one file from some directory.

All documents have the following format: the first line contains the document identifier, the second line contains the name of the document, all subsequent lines contain the text of the document. The document identifier is used to make installing the problem more convenient and has no useful information for the participants.

You need to write a program that should indicate the subject for a given document. It is guaranteed that all documents given as input to your program correspond to one of the three subjects of the training set.

Input

The first line contains integer id (0 ≤ id ≤ 106) — the document identifier. The second line contains the name of the document. The third and the subsequent lines contain the text of the document. It is guaranteed that the size of any given document will not exceed 10 kilobytes.

The tests for this problem are divided into 10 groups. Documents of groups 1 and 2 are taken from the training set, but their identifiers will not match the identifiers specified in the training set. Groups from the 3-rd to the 10-th are roughly sorted by the author in ascending order of difficulty (these groups contain documents which aren't present in the training set).

Output

Print an integer from 1 to 3, inclusive — the number of the subject the given document corresponds to.

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have documents categorized into 3 subjects (1, 2, or 3)
2. Each document has an identifier, name, and text content
3. We need to classify a new document into one of the three subjects
4. The training set contains documents already categorized by subject
5. We need to build a classifier (likely using text analysis) to determine which subject a document belongs to

## Approach

Since we don't have direct access to the training data files, I need to think about how to create a classification system:

1. **Feature extraction**: Extract meaningful features from the document text
2. **Pattern recognition**: Identify characteristic words or phrases for each subject
3. **Classification**: Assign the document to the subject that best matches its features

Given that this is a text classification problem with only 3 classes, I'll use a simple approach:

- Extract words from the document text
- Count frequency of words in each subject based on training data patterns
- Use the most characteristic words for each subject to classify new documents

However, since I can't access the actual training data, I'll implement a basic text classification system that looks for characteristic patterns in documents.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read the input document (id, name, text)
2. Process the text content to extract features
3. Classify based on the most likely subject

The key insight: documents from different subjects likely contain different keywords or patterns.

I'll implement a simple approach that:
- Looks for common keywords that appear in subject-specific documents
- Uses a rule-based system with keyword matching

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def classify_document():
    # Read input
    try:
        lines = []
        for line in sys.stdin:
            lines.append(line.strip())
        
        if not lines:
            return 1  # default if no input
        
        # Parse the input
        doc_id = int(lines[0]) if lines[0] else 0
        doc_name = lines[1] if len(lines) > 1 else ""
        text_content = " ".join(lines[2:]) if len(lines) > 2 else ""
        
        # Clean text content
        text_content = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', text_content.lower())
        words = text_content.split()
        
        # Simple rule-based classification based on common patterns
        # This is a placeholder - in a real scenario, we'd train on actual training data
        
        # Count word frequencies
        word_count = Counter(words)
        
        # Define characteristic words for each subject (these would be learned from training data)
        # For example: subject 1 might have words like "education", "school", "student"
        # subject 2 might have "technology", "computer", "software"
        # subject 3 might have "trade", "business", "market"
        
        # This is a simplified version - in practice, we'd use actual training data
        # to determine these patterns
        
        # Subject 1: education, school, student, learning, academic
        subject1_keywords = ['education', 'school', 'student', 'learning', 'academic', 'teacher', 'university', 'class', 'exam']
        
        # Subject 2: technology, computer, software, programming, internet, network
        subject2_keywords = ['technology', 'computer', 'software', 'programming', 'internet', 'network', 'digital', 'hardware', 'data']
        
        # Subject 3: trade, business, market, commerce, economy, product, price, supply
        subject3_keywords = ['trade', 'business', 'market', 'commerce', 'economy', 'product', 'price', 'supply', 'demand']
        
        # Score each subject based on keyword matches
        score_1 = sum(1 for word in subject1_keywords if word in words)
        score_2 = sum(1 for word in subject2_keywords if word in words)
        score_3 = sum(1 for word in subject3_keywords if word in words)
        
        # Add weight based on word frequency (more common words give higher scores)
        for word in words:
            if word in subject1_keywords:
                score_1 += 1
            elif word in subject2_keywords:
                score_2 += 1
            elif word in subject3_keywords:
                score_3 += 1
        
        # In a real scenario, we'd use trained models or TF-IDF
        # For now, just use simple scoring
        
        # Fallback to most frequent subject
        max_score = max(score_1, score_2, score_3)
        
        if max_score == score_1:
            return 1
        elif max_score == score_2:
            return 2
        else:
            return 3
            
    except Exception as e:
        # Fallback in case of errors
        return 1

# Run the classification
result = classify_document()
print(result)
```

```python
        return

        print(1)
        return

    subject3_keywords = ['trade', 'business', 'market', 'commerce', 'economy', 'product', 'price', 'supply', 'demand']

    score_2 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject2_keywords)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a document classification problem where each document belongs to one of 3 subjects (1, 2, or 3)
2. We're given a training set with documents organized by subject in directories "1", "2", and "3"
3. For a new document, we need to predict which subject it belongs to
4. The training set is available, but we can't directly access it in this problem
5. We need to implement a document classification algorithm

## Solution Approach

Since we don't have direct access to the training data in this environment, I'll implement a reasonable approach:

1. **Feature extraction**: Extract relevant text features from the document
2. **Classification**: Use a simple rule-based or statistical approach to classify documents

Given that we don't have the actual training data, I'll use a simple approach that looks at the text content:

### Key observations:
- Documents with subject 1 might have certain keywords or patterns
- Documents with subject 2 might have different patterns
- Documents with subject 3 might have other patterns

Since we don't have the actual training data, I'll implement a basic approach that:
1. Extracts keywords and patterns from the document text
2. Uses simple heuristics to classify based on common words or patterns

## Implementation Strategy

I'll use a simple keyword-based approach with counts of common words that might be associated with each subject. Since we don't have the training data, I'll implement a basic classifier with common patterns.

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter

def classify_document():
    # Read input
    try:
        lines = []
        for line in sys.stdin:
            lines.append(line.strip())
        
        if len(lines) == 0:
            return 1  # Default fallback
        
        # Extract document identifier and name
        doc_id = int(lines[0]) if lines[0].isdigit() else 0
        doc_name = lines[1] if len(lines) > 1 else ""
        
        # Extract document text (all lines after the name)
        text = " ".join(lines[2:]) if len(lines) > 2 else ""
        
        if not text:
            return 1  # Default if no text
        
        # Convert text to lowercase for case-insensitive analysis
        text = text.lower()
        
        # Simple keyword-based classification
        # This is a placeholder - in a real scenario, we would train on actual data
        # Here we use some basic heuristics based on common patterns
        
        # Count occurrences of keywords associated with each subject
        keywords = {
            1: ["education", "school", "class", "student", "teacher", "learning", "course"],
            2: ["technology", "computer", "software", "programming", "code", "digital", "network"],
            3: ["trade", "business", "market", "commerce", "sales", "profit", "investment"]
        }
        
        # Count frequency of keywords
        keyword_count = {}
        for subject, subject_keywords in keywords.items():
            count = 0
            for keyword in subject_keywords:
                count += text.count(keyword.lower())
            keyword_count[subject] = count
        
        # Determine the subject with most matching keywords
        max_count = max(keyword_count.values())
        
        # If all subjects have same count, use some heuristic
        if max_count == 0:
            # Fallback to simple pattern recognition
            if "market" in text or "business" in text or "trade" in text:
                return 3
            elif "computer" in text or "code" in text or "programming" in text:
                return 2
            elif "education" in text or "school" in text or "student" in text:
                return 1
            else:
                return 1  # Default
        
        # Return the subject with the highest keyword count
        for subject, count in keyword_count.items():
            if count == max_count:
                return subject
                
    except Exception as e:
        # Fallback to default
        return 1

# Execute the classification
result = classify_document()
print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to classify a document into one of three subjects (1, 2, or 3)
2. We have a training set with documents categorized by subject (directories 1, 2, 3)
3. For each document, we need to determine which subject it belongs to based on its text content
4. The documents are in a specific format: ID, name, then text content
5. We need to build a classifier that can assign subjects based on text features

## Approach

Since we don't have direct access to the training data, I need to implement a document classification system. The most straightforward approach is to use basic text analysis features like:
- Word frequency analysis
- Presence of common words in each subject
- Text pattern recognition

Given that the training data is available at the link, I'll assume we can access it and create a simple classification model.

## Solution Strategy

1. Extract features from the document text (e.g., word frequency, common keywords)
2. Compare these features with patterns observed in the training data
3. Assign the subject based on which subject's patterns match best

Since we don't have actual access to the training data, I'll implement a simple rule-based classifier that:
- Looks for common keywords associated with each subject
- Uses frequency-based analysis

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def extract_features(text):
    """Extract useful features from document text"""
    # Convert to lowercase and remove punctuation
    text = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', text.lower())
    
    # Split into words
    words = text.split()
    
    # Remove very short words
    words = [word for word in words if len(word) > 2]
    
    # Get word frequency
    word_count = Counter(words)
    
    # Extract features
    features = {
        'total_words': len(words),
        'unique_words': len(word_count),
        'common_words': word_count.most_common(10),
        'avg_word_length': sum(len(word) for word in words) / len(words) if words else 0
    }
    
    return features

def classify_document(text, training_data):
    """Classify document based on training data patterns"""
    # For demo purposes, let's create some heuristics based on common patterns
    # In a real scenario, we would train on the actual training data
    
    # Extract features
    features = extract_features(text)
    
    # Simple heuristics:
    # Subject 1 might contain more technical terms
    # Subject 2 might contain more administrative terms
    # Subject 3 might contain more economic/trade terms
    
    # Look for common keywords in each subject
    subject_keywords = {
        1: ['technical', 'system', 'algorithm', 'code', 'function', 'process', 'logic', 'structure'],
        2: ['management', 'policy', 'administrative', 'procedure', 'rule', 'governance', 'protocol', 'office'],
        3: ['trade', 'market', 'business', 'economics', 'commodity', 'export', 'import', 'profit', 'investment']
    }
    
    # Score for each subject
    scores = {1: 0, 2: 0, 3: 0}
    
    for subject, keywords in subject_keywords.items():
        for keyword in keywords:
            # Check if keyword appears in text
            if keyword in text.lower():
                scores[subject] += 10
        
        # Bonus for longer texts in subject 1 (technical documents)
        if subject == 1 and features['total_words'] > 50:
            scores[subject] += 5
            
        # Bonus for administrative terms in subject 2
        if subject == 2 and 'policy' in text.lower() or 'management' in text.lower():
            scores[subject] += 8
            
        # Bonus for trade/economic terms in subject 3
        if subject == 3 and features['total_words'] > 30:
            scores[subject] += 7
    
    # Also consider word frequency - more variety in subject 2
    if features['unique_words'] > 50:
        scores[2] += 10
    
    # Return the subject with highest score
    return max(scores, key=scores.get)

def main():
    # Read input
    try:
        id_input = input().strip()
        name = input().strip()
        
        # Read all remaining lines as text
        text_lines = []
        try:
            while True:
                line = input().strip()
                if line == '':
                    break
                text_lines.append(line)
        except EOFError:
            pass
        
        # Combine all text lines
        full_text = ' '.join(text_lines)
        
        # Classify the document
        subject = classify_document(full_text, {})
        
        print(subject)
        
    except Exception as e:
        # Fallback - if something goes wrong, default to subject 1
        print(1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Wait, I see that this approach is quite simplified and might not be accurate enough. Let me reconsider.

Since we have access to a training set, a more robust approach would be to build a classifier using the actual training data. However, without being able to access it directly, I'll implement a more sophisticated solution that extracts meaningful features.

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def analyze_text(text):
    """Extract detailed text features"""
    text = text.lower()
    
    # Remove punctuation and numbers
    text = re.sub(r'[^a-z\s]', '', text)
    
    words = text.split()
    
    # Remove very short words
    words = [word for word in words if len(word) >= 3]
    
    if not words:
        return {'subject': 1}  # Default to subject 1
    
    word_freq = Counter(words)
    
    # Calculate statistics
    total_words = len(words)
    unique_words = len(word_freq)
    
    # Identify common patterns
    tech_keywords = ['system', 'code', 'algorithm', 'function', 'engine', 'database', 'network']
    admin_keywords = ['management', 'policy', 'procedure', 'office', 'governance', 'rule', 'administration']
    trade_keywords = ['trade', 'market', 'economy', 'business', 'investment', 'commodity', 'profit', 'marketplace']
    
    # Score for each subject
    scores = {1: 0, 2: 0, 3: 0}
    
    # Weighted scoring based on keywords
    for word in tech_keywords:
        scores[1] += word_freq.get(word, 0) * 10
    
    for word in admin_keywords:
        scores[2] += word_freq.get(word, 0) * 10
    
    for word in trade_keywords:
        scores[3] += word_freq.get(word, 0) * 10
    
    # Additional factors
    # Subject 1: longer documents with technical terms
    if scores[1] > 0 and total_words > 20:
        scores[1] += 5
    
    # Subject 2: documents with multiple administrative terms
    if scores[2] > 0 and unique_words > 30:
        scores[2] += 8
    
    # Subject 3: documents with economic terms and longer texts
    if scores[3] > 0 and total_words > 30:
        scores[3] += 7
    
    # Final decision
    return scores

def main():
    # Read document inputs
    try:
        id_input = input().strip()
        name = input().strip()
        
        # Read text content
        text_lines = []
        try:
            while True:
                line = input().strip()
                if line == '':
                    break
                text_lines.append(line)
        except EOFError:
            # EOF reached, no more input
            pass
        
        full_text = ' '.join(text_lines)
        
        # Analyze the text
        scores = analyze_text(full_text)
        
        # Determine subject with highest score
        subject = 1
        if scores[2] > scores[1] and scores[2] > scores[3]:
            subject = 2
        elif scores[3] > scores[1] and scores[3] > scores[2]:
            subject = 3
        
        print(subject)
        
    except Exception as e:
        # Safety fallback
        print(1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def analyze_text(text):
    """Extract detailed text features"""
    text = text.lower()
    
    # Remove punctuation and numbers
    text = re.sub(r'[^a-z\s]', '', text)
    
    words = text.split()
    
    # Remove very short words
    words = [word for word in words if len(word) >= 3]
    
    if not words:
        return {'subject': 1}  # Default to subject 1
    
    word_freq = Counter(words)
    
    # Calculate statistics
    total_words = len(words)
    unique_words = len(word_freq)
    
    # Identify common patterns
    tech_keywords = ['system', 'code', 'algorithm', 'function', 'engine', 'database', 'network']
    admin_keywords = ['management', 'policy', 'procedure', 'office', 'governance', 'rule', 'administration']
    trade_keywords = ['trade', 'market', 'economy', 'business', 'investment', 'commodity', 'profit', 'marketplace']
    
    # Score for each subject
    scores = {1: 0, 2: 0, 3: 0}
    
    # Weighted scoring based on keywords
    for word in tech_keywords:
        scores[1] += word_freq.get(word, 0) * 10
    
    for word in admin_keywords:
        scores[2] += word_freq.get(word, 0) * 10
    
    for word in trade_keywords:
        scores[3] += word_freq.get(word, 0) * 10
    
    # Additional factors
    # Subject 1: longer documents with technical terms
    if scores[1] > 0 and total_words > 20:
        scores[1] += 5
    
    # Subject 2: documents with multiple administrative terms
    if scores[2] > 0 and unique_words > 30:
        scores[2] += 8
    
    # Subject 3: documents with economic terms and longer texts
    if scores[3] > 0 and total_words > 30:
        scores[3] += 7
    
    # Final decision
    return scores

def main():
    # Read document inputs
    try:
        id_input = input().strip()
        name = input().strip()
        
        # Read text content
        text_lines = []
        try:
            while True:
                line = input().strip()
                if line == '':
                    break
                text_lines.append(line)
        except EOFError:
            # EOF reached, no more input
            pass
        
        full_text = ' '.join(text_lines)
        
        # Analyze the text
        scores = analyze_text(full_text)
        
        # Determine subject with highest score
        subject = 1
        if scores[2] > scores[1] and scores[2] > scores[3]:
            subject = 2
        elif scores[3] > scores[1] and scores[3] > scores[2]:
            subject = 3
        
        print(subject)
        
    except Exception as e:
        # Safety fallback
        print(1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The Smart Beaver from ABBYY came up with another splendid problem for the ABBYY Cup participants! This time the Beaver invites the contest participants to check out a problem on sorting documents by their subjects. Let's describe the problem:

You've got some training set of documents. For each document you know its subject. The subject in this problem is an integer from 1 to 3. Each of these numbers has a physical meaning. For instance, all documents with subject 3 are about trade.

You can download the training set of documents at the following link: http://download4.abbyy.com/a2/X2RZ2ZWXBG5VYWAL61H76ZQM/train.zip. The archive contains three directories with names "1", "2", "3". Directory named "1" contains documents on the 1-st subject, directory "2" contains documents on the 2-nd subject, and directory "3" contains documents on the 3-rd subject. Each document corresponds to exactly one file from some directory.

All documents have the following format: the first line contains the document identifier, the second line contains the name of the document, all subsequent lines contain the text of the document. The document identifier is used to make installing the problem more convenient and has no useful information for the participants.

You need to write a program that should indicate the subject for a given document. It is guaranteed that all documents given as input to your program correspond to one of the three subjects of the training set.

Input

The first line contains integer id (0 ≤ id ≤ 106) — the document identifier. The second line contains the name of the document. The third and the subsequent lines contain the text of the document. It is guaranteed that the size of any given document will not exceed 10 kilobytes.

The tests for this problem are divided into 10 groups. Documents of groups 1 and 2 are taken from the training set, but their identifiers will not match the identifiers specified in the training set. Groups from the 3-rd to the 10-th are roughly sorted by the author in ascending order of difficulty (these groups contain documents which aren't present in the training set).

Output

Print an integer from 1 to 3, inclusive — the number of the subject the given document corresponds to.

Examples

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

## Solution Approach

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def extract_features(text):
    """Extract basic features from text"""
    features = {}
    
    # Convert to lowercase
    text = text.lower()
    
    # Word frequency
    words = re.findall(r'\b[a-zA-Z]+\b', text)
    features['word_count'] = len(words)
    features['unique_words'] = len(set(words))
    
    # Character frequency
    char_count = Counter(text)
    features['char_count'] = len(text)
    
    # Common patterns in different subjects
    features['has_number'] = bool(re.search(r'\d', text))
    features['has_money'] = bool(re.search(r'\b\d+\.?\d*\s*(dollars|pounds|euros|rupees|cents)\b', text, re.IGNORECASE))
    features['has_budget'] = bool(re.search(r'\b(budget|expense|income|revenue|profit|loss)\b', text, re.IGNORECASE))
    features['has_company'] = bool(re.search(r'\b(company|firm|corporation|organization|business)\b', text, re.IGNORECASE))
    features['has_trade'] = bool(re.search(r'\b(trade|market|purchase|sale|stock|investment)\b', text, re.IGNORECASE))
    features['has_personal'] = bool(re.search(r'\b(person|individual|family|home|personal)\b', text, re.IGNORECASE))
    features['has_education'] = bool(re.search(r'\b(education|school|student|teacher|learning)\b', text, re.IGNORECASE))
    
    return features

def classify_document(text, features_dict):
    """
    Simple classification based on feature patterns
    This is a placeholder - in reality we would train on the actual training set
    """
    # Based on the analysis of what might be in different subjects
    # Subject 1: Personal/individual matters
    # Subject 2: Education/academic matters
    # Subject 3: Trade/business matters
    
    has_personal = features_dict.get('has_personal', False)
    has_education = features_dict.get('has_education', False)
    has_trade = features_dict.get('has_trade', False)
    has_money = features_dict.get('has_money', False)
    has_budget = features_dict.get('has_budget', False)
    has_company = features_dict.get('has_company', False)
    
    # Simple rule-based classification
    # Subject 3: Trade/business (money, trade, company, budget)
    if has_trade or has_money or has_budget or has_company:
        return 3
    
    # Subject 2: Education/academic (school, student, learning)
    if has_education:
        return 2
    
    # Subject 1: Personal/individual (person, family, home)
    if has_personal:
        return 1
    
    # Default to subject 1 if no clear pattern
    return 1

# Read input
lines = []
try:
    while True:
        line = input()
        if line == '':
            break
        lines.append(line)
except EOFError:
    pass

# Extract the document text (all lines after the first two)
document_id = lines[0] if lines else ""
document_name = lines[1] if len(lines) > 1 else ""
text_content = ""
if len(lines) > 2:
    text_content = "\n".join(lines[2:])

# If no text content, use default
if not text_content.strip():
    text_content = ""

# Extract features
features = extract_features(text_content)

# Classify the document
subject = classify_document(text_content, features)

print(subject)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have documents classified into 3 subjects (1, 2, or 3)
2. We need to predict which subject a given document belongs to
3. We have a training set with documents already labeled by subject
4. We need to build a classifier that can assign subjects to new documents

## Approach

Since this is a document classification problem, I'll use a simple approach:

1. **Collect training data**: Extract all documents from the training set directories
2. **Build feature representations**: Create features from the document text (word frequency, presence of keywords, etc.)
3. **Train a simple classifier**: Since the problem is small (3 classes), I'll use a rule-based approach or simple statistical method

Given that I don't have direct access to the training files, I'll need to implement a general solution that would work with the training data.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Parse input**: Read document ID, name, and text
2. **Create features**: Extract common features from text (word counts, character frequencies, etc.)
3. **Classify**: Use a simple rule-based classification that might work well for this constrained problem

Let me implement a solution that uses simple heuristics based on common text patterns:

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def extract_features(text):
    """Extract simple features from text"""
    # Convert to lowercase and remove punctuation
    text = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', text.lower())
    
    # Basic features
    words = text.split()
    word_count = len(words)
    unique_words = len(set(words))
    
    # Common words that might appear in each subject
    tech_words = ['technology', 'system', 'computer', 'software', 'code', 'algorithm', 'data']
    business_words = ['business', 'company', 'market', 'trade', 'profit', 'investment', 'finance']
    general_words = ['document', 'file', 'information', 'content', 'text', 'page', 'example']
    
    tech_score = sum(1 for word in words if word in tech_words)
    business_score = sum(1 for word in words if word in business_words)
    general_score = sum(1 for word in words if word in general_words)
    
    # Statistical features
    avg_word_length = sum(len(word) for word in words) / word_count if word_count > 0 else 0
    vowel_count = sum(sum(1 for char in word if char in 'aeiou') for word in words)
    
    return {
        'word_count': word_count,
        'unique_words': unique_words,
        'tech_score': tech_score,
        'business_score': business_score,
        'general_score': general_score,
        'avg_word_length': avg_word_length,
        'vowel_count': vowel_count
    }

def classify_document(text_features):
    """Classify document based on features"""
    # Simple rule-based classification
    # If tech-like words dominate, assign to subject 1 (technology)
    # If business-like words dominate, assign to subject 2 (trade/business)
    # Otherwise, assign to subject 3 (general)
    
    # Use a weighted approach
    tech_score = text_features['tech_score']
    business_score = text_features['business_score']
    
    # Simple threshold-based approach
    if tech_score > business_score and tech_score > 0:
        return 1
    elif business_score > tech_score and business_score > 0:
        return 2
    else:
        # Default to subject 3 (general)
        return 3

def main():
    # Read input
    try:
        # First line: document ID
        id_line = input().strip()
        if not id_line:
            id_line = "0"
            
        # Second line: document name
        name_line = input().strip()
        
        # Remaining lines: document text
        text_lines = []
        try:
            while True:
                line = input().strip()
                if line:
                    text_lines.append(line)
        except EOFError:
            pass
            
        # Combine all text lines
        full_text = " ".join(text_lines) if text_lines else ""
        
        # If no text, default to subject 3
        if not full_text:
            print(3)
            return
            
        # Extract features
        features = extract_features(full_text)
        
        # Classify
        subject = classify_document(features)
        print(subject)
        
    except Exception as e:
        # Fallback - if anything goes wrong, assign to subject 3
        print(3)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

```python
import sys
import re

def get_document_features(text):
    """Extract features from document text"""
    # Clean text
    text = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', text.lower())
    words = text.split()
    
    # Basic feature extraction
    word_count = len(words)
    unique_words = len(set(words))
    
    # Simple heuristics for subjects
    tech_keywords = ['technology', 'software', 'code', 'computer', 'system', 'internet', 'data', 'algorithm']
    business_keywords = ['business', 'company', 'market', 'trade', 'profit', 'investment', 'finance', 'economy']
    general_keywords = ['document', 'information', 'text', 'content', 'page', 'file', 'example']
    
    tech_count = sum(1 for word in words if word in tech_keywords)
    business_count = sum(1 for word in words if word in business_keywords)
    general_count = sum(1 for word in words if word in general_keywords)
    
    # Simple classification rule
    if tech_count > business_count and tech_count > 0:
        return 1
    elif business_count > tech_count and business_count > 0:
        return 2
    else:
        return 3

# Read input
try:
    # Document ID
    doc_id = input().strip()
    
    # Document name
    doc_name = input().strip()
    
    # Document text
    text = ""
    try:
        while True:
            line = input().strip()
            if line:
                text += " " + line
    except EOFError:
        pass
    
    # If no text, default to subject 3
    if not text.strip():
        print(3)
    else:
        # Classify based on text content
        subject = get_document_features(text)
        print(subject)
        
except Exception as e:
    # In case of any error, default to subject 3
    print(3)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a document classification problem where we need to determine which subject (1, 2, or 3) a document belongs to
2. We're given a training set with documents organized by subject (directories 1, 2, 3)
3. For a new document, we need to predict its subject based on its text content
4. The documents have a specific format: first line is ID, second line is name, then text content
5. We need to classify unseen documents (some from training set, some not)

## Solution Approach

Since we don't have access to the actual training data files, this is a classic text classification problem. We need to implement a document classification system.

The key insight is that we need to build a classifier that can recognize patterns in the text to determine which subject a document belongs to. Since we don't have access to the training data, we need to implement a general approach.

I'll use a simple but effective approach:
1. Extract features from the document text (word frequencies, n-gram patterns)
2. Train a classifier on the training data (though we can't access it directly)
3. For the given document, predict the subject based on its content

However, since we don't have actual training data, I'll implement a heuristic-based approach that looks at common words or patterns in each subject.

## Alternative Approach (Most Practical)

Since we can't access the training data directly, and this is a programming competition problem, the expected solution is likely to be based on analyzing common words or patterns in each subject. I'll implement a simple rule-based classifier that looks for characteristic words in each subject.

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter

def classify_document(text):
    """
    Classify a document based on its text content
    This is a heuristic-based approach that looks for common patterns
    """
    # Convert text to lowercase and split into words
    words = text.lower().split()
    
    # Remove punctuation and clean words
    cleaned_words = []
    for word in words:
        # Remove punctuation
        cleaned = ''.join(char for char in word if char.isalnum())
        if cleaned:
            cleaned_words.append(cleaned)
    
    # Count word frequencies
    word_counts = Counter(cleaned_words)
    
    # Define characteristic words for each subject (these would come from training data)
    # This is a placeholder - in a real scenario, we would have learned these from training
    # Subject 1: business, market, trade, commerce, finance, etc.
    # Subject 2: technology, software, code, programming, development, etc.
    # Subject 3: trade, goods, products, supply, demand, market
    
    # Heuristic for subject 1 (business/trade)
    subject_1_words = {'business', 'market', 'trade', 'commerce', 'finance', 'profit', 'investment', 'company'}
    
    # Heuristic for subject 2 (technology)
    subject_2_words = {'technology', 'software', 'code', 'programming', 'development', 'algorithm', 'network', 'server'}
    
    # Heuristic for subject 3 (trade/goods)
    subject_3_words = {'goods', 'products', 'supply', 'demand', 'purchase', 'sell', 'inventory', 'warehouse'}
    
    # Calculate scores for each subject
    score_1 = sum(1 for word in cleaned_words if word in subject_1_words)
    score_2 = sum(1 for word in cleaned_words if word in subject_2_words)
    score_3 = sum(1 for word in cleaned_words if word in subject_3_words)
    
    # Use the highest score to determine subject
    if score_1 >= score_2 and score_1 >= score_3:
        return 1
    elif score_2 >= score_1 and score_2 >= score_3:
        return 2
    else:
        return 3

# Read input
lines = []
try:
    while True:
        line = input()
        lines.append(line)
except EOFError:
    pass

# Extract text content (all lines after the first two)
id_line = lines[0]
name_line = lines[1]
text_content = ' '.join(lines[2:]) if len(lines) > 2 else ""

# Classify the document
subject = classify_document(text_content)

# Output the result
print(subject)
```

This solution:
1. Reads the document input line by line
2. Extracts the text content (all lines after the ID and name)
3. Processes the text to clean it and extract words
4. Uses heuristic word patterns to classify based on which subject's characteristic words appear more frequently
5. Returns the subject number (1, 2, or 3)

Note: In a real competition scenario, the characteristic words would be learned from the actual training data. This solution provides a reasonable approximation based on the problem description and typical document classification patterns.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The Smart Beaver from ABBYY came up with another splendid problem for the ABBYY Cup participants! This time the Beaver invites the contest participants to check out a problem on sorting documents by their subjects. Let's describe the problem:

You've got some training set of documents. For each document you know its subject. The subject in this problem is an integer from 1 to 3. Each of these numbers has a physical meaning. For instance, all documents with subject 3 are about trade.

You can download the training set of documents at the following link: http://download4.abbyy.com/a2/X2RZ2ZWXBG5VYWAL61H76ZQM/train.zip. The archive contains three directories with names "1", "2", "3". Directory named "1" contains documents on the 1-st subject, directory "2" contains documents on the 2-nd subject, and directory "3" contains documents on the 3-rd subject. Each document corresponds to exactly one file from some directory.

All documents have the following format: the first line contains the document identifier, the second line contains the name of the document, all subsequent lines contain the text of the document. The document identifier is used to make installing the problem more convenient and has no useful information for the participants.

You need to write a program that should indicate the subject for a given document. It is guaranteed that all documents given as input to your program correspond to one of the three subjects of the training set.

Input

The first line contains integer id (0 ≤ id ≤ 106) — the document identifier. The second line contains the name of the document. The third and the subsequent lines contain the text of the document. It is guaranteed that the size of any given document will not exceed 10 kilobytes.

The tests for this problem are divided into 10 groups. Documents of groups 1 and 2 are taken from the training set, but their identifiers will not match the identifiers specified in the training set. Groups from the 3-rd to the 10-th are roughly sorted by the author in ascending order of difficulty (these groups contain documents which aren't present in the training set).

Output

Print an integer from 1 to 3, inclusive — the number of the subject the given document corresponds to.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to classify a document into one of three subjects (1, 2, or 3) based on its content
2. We have a training set with documents organized by subject (directories 1, 2, 3)
3. For each document, we get:
   - Document identifier (not useful)
   - Document name
   - Document text (multiple lines)
4. We need to predict the subject of a given document

## Approach

Since we don't have access to the training set files directly, I need to think about how to implement a document classification system. The problem is about text classification, so I'll use machine learning techniques.

Key considerations:
- We need to build a classifier that can recognize patterns in text to determine the subject
- Since the training data is not provided here, I'll need to create a reasonable approach
- The documents are likely to contain keywords or phrases specific to each subject

## Solution Strategy

I'll use a simple rule-based approach with keyword matching, as this is a common approach for document classification when training data isn't directly accessible:

1. Extract common keywords or phrases that are characteristic of each subject
2. For each document, count the frequency of keywords from each subject
3. Assign the subject with the highest keyword score

Since we don't have access to the actual training data, I'll implement a generic solution that looks for common patterns. In a real scenario, I would train on the training set to find actual patterns.

## Implementation

```python
import re
from collections import Counter

def classify_document():
    # Read input
    doc_id = input().strip()
    doc_name = input().strip()
    
    # Read document text
    text = ""
    try:
        while True:
            line = input().strip()
            if line == "":
                break
            text += line + " "
    except EOFError:
        pass
    
    # Clean text
    text = text.lower()
    text = re.sub(r'[^a-z\s]', '', text)  # Keep only letters and spaces
    
    # Define characteristic keywords for each subject
    # These would be learned from the training set in a real scenario
    # For now, using reasonable assumptions based on subject meaning
    subject_1_keywords = [
        'technology', 'computer', 'programming', 'software', 'code', 'development', 
        'engineer', 'algorithm', 'data', 'processing', 'mathematics', 'solution'
    ]
    
    subject_2_keywords = [
        'education', 'school', 'class', 'teacher', 'student', 'learning', 'academic', 
        'curriculum', 'examination', 'research', 'university', 'knowledge'
    ]
    
    subject_3_keywords = [
        'trade', 'commerce', 'business', 'market', 'economy', 'industry', 'company', 
        'investment', 'profit', 'sales', 'purchase', 'pricing'
    ]
    
    # Count keyword frequencies
    def count_keywords(text, keywords):
        counts = {}
        for kw in keywords:
            pattern = r'\b' + re.escape(kw) + r'\b'
            matches = re.findall(pattern, text)
            counts[kw] = len(matches)
        return sum(counts.values()) if counts else 0
    
    # Calculate scores for each subject
    score_1 = count_keywords(text, subject_1_keywords)
    score_2 = count_keywords(text, subject_2_keywords)
    score_3 = count_keywords(text, subject_3_keywords)
    
    # Simple rule: assign subject with highest score
    max_score = max(score_1, score_2, score_3)
    
    if score_1 == max_score:
        return 1
    elif score_2 == max_score:
        return 2
    else:
        return 3

# Execute classification
result = classify_document()
print(result)
```

## Important Notes

1. This solution uses keyword-based classification which is a simple heuristic
2. In a real competition with actual training data, I would:
   - Download and parse the training set
   - Use machine learning (e.g., Naive Bayes, SVM) to learn the actual patterns
   - Train on the training set to create a proper classifier
3. The keywords used here are generic and would need to be adjusted based on the actual content of the training documents

Since the actual training set is not accessible in this environment, this solution provides a reasonable implementation that would work with a properly trained model if the training data were available.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to classify documents into one of three subjects (1, 2, or 3)
2. We have a training set with documents already labeled by subject
3. For a new document, we need to predict which subject it belongs to
4. The training set contains directories "1", "2", "3" with documents of each subject
5. We need to build a classifier to predict the subject of new documents

## Approach

Since this is a document classification problem, I'll use a simple feature-based approach:

1. **Extract features** from the document text (words, frequency, patterns)
2. **Train a classifier** on the training data to learn patterns for each subject
3. **Predict** the subject for the new document

Given that we don't have access to the actual training data file, I'll need to implement a reasonable approach based on common document classification techniques.

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Feature Extraction
I'll extract important features from the document text:
- Word frequencies
- Common words in each subject
- Length of document
- Character patterns

### Step 2: Classification Strategy
Since I can't access the actual training data, I'll use a simple rule-based approach:
- Count words in the document
- Look for common patterns that might indicate the subject
- Use word frequency as a proxy for subject classification

### Step 3: Implementation

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def preprocess_text(text):
    """Clean and preprocess text"""
    text = text.lower()
    # Remove punctuation and digits
    text = re.sub(r'[^a-z\s]', '', text)
    # Split into words
    words = text.split()
    # Remove empty strings
    words = [word for word in words if word]
    return words

def get_document_features(text):
    """Extract features from document text"""
    words = preprocess_text(text)
    if not words:
        return {"length": 0, "word_count": 0, "unique_words": 0}
    
    word_count = len(words)
    unique_words = len(set(words))
    avg_word_length = sum(len(word) for word in words) / word_count if word_count > 0 else 0
    
    # Count frequency of common words (this would be trained on actual data)
    # For now, we'll use a simple heuristic
    common_words = {
        'subject': ['trade', 'market', 'business', 'economy', 'stock', 'share'],
        'subject2': ['education', 'school', 'student', 'teacher', 'class', 'learning'],
        'subject3': ['technology', 'computer', 'software', 'internet', 'programming', 'code']
    }
    
    features = {
        'length': len(text),
        'word_count': word_count,
        'unique_words': unique_words,
        'avg_word_length': avg_word_length
    }
    
    # Check if any common words are present
    for subject, word_list in common_words.items():
        matches = [word for word in words if word in word_list]
        features[f'has_subject_{subject}'] = len(matches) > 0
    
    return features

def classify_document(text):
    """Classify document based on features"""
    features = get_document_features(text)
    
    # Simple rule-based classification - this would be improved with actual training data
    # In a real scenario, we would train a model on the actual training set
    
    # Check for subject-specific words
    text_lower = text.lower()
    
    # Subject 1: Trade/Market/Business/Economy
    trade_words = ['trade', 'market', 'business', 'economy', 'stock', 'share', 'investment']
    if any(word in text_lower for word in trade_words):
        return 1
    
    # Subject 2: Education/School/Student
    edu_words = ['education', 'school', 'student', 'teacher', 'class', 'learning', 'university']
    if any(word in text_lower for word in edu_words):
        return 2
    
    # Subject 3: Technology/Computer
    tech_words = ['technology', 'computer', 'software', 'internet', 'programming', 'code', 'software']
    if any(word in text_lower for word in tech_words):
        return 3
    
    # Fallback: use word count and length as heuristic
    # Longer documents might be more technical (subject 3)
    # Shorter documents might be educational (subject 2)
    if features['word_count'] > 50:
        return 3
    elif features['word_count'] < 10:
        return 2
    else:
        return 1

# Read input
lines = []
try:
    while True:
        line = input()
        lines.append(line)
except EOFError:
    pass

# Extract document data
doc_id = int(lines[0])
doc_name = lines[1]
doc_text = '\n'.join(lines[2:])

# Classify the document
subject = classify_document(doc_text)

print(subject)
```

This solution uses a rule-based approach that looks for subject-specific keywords in the document text. In a real-world scenario with access to the training data, we would:

1. Build a frequency model for each subject
2. Use machine learning (like Naive Bayes or Logistic Regression) to classify documents
3. Train on the actual training set to get better accuracy

The current implementation works as a baseline that might perform reasonably well on the given test cases.

        'subject2': ['education', 'school', 'student', 'teacher', 'class', 'learning'],

        'length': len(text),
        'word_count': word_count,
        'unique_words': unique_words,
        'avg_word_length': avg_word_length
    }

    text_lower = text.lower()

    if any(word in text_lower for word in tech_words):
        return 3
    
    # Fallback: use word count and length as heuristic
    # Longer documents might be more technical (subject 3)
    # Shorter documents might be educational (subject 2)
    if features['word_count'] > 50:
        return 3
    elif features['word_count'] < 10:
        return 2
    else:
        return 1
